An Inexact Projected Gradient Method with Rounding and Lifting by Nonlinear Programming for Solving Rank-One Semidefinite Relaxation of Polynomial Optimization
Heng Yang, Ling Liang Luca Carlone, Kim-Chuan Toh

Abstract
We consider solving high-order semidefinite programming (SDP) relaxations of nonconvex polynomial optimization problems (POPs) that often admit degenerate rank-one optimal solutions. Instead of solving the SDP alone, we propose a new algorithmic framework that blends local search using the nonconvex POP into global descent using the convex SDP. In particular, we first design a globally convergent inexact projected gradient method (iPGM) for solving the SDP that serves as the backbone of our framework. We then accelerate iPGM by taking long, but safeguarded , rank-one steps generated by fast nonlinear programming algorithms. We prove that the new framework is still globally convergent for solving the SDP. To solve the iPGM subproblem of projecting a given point onto the feasible set of the SDP, we design a two-phase algorithm with phase one using a symmetric Gauss-Seidel based accelerated proximal gradient method (sGS-APG) to generate a good initial point, and phase two using a modified limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS) method to obtain an accurate solution. We analyze the convergence for both phases and establish a novel global convergence result for the modified L-BFGS that does not require the objective function to be twice continuously differentiable. We conduct numerical experiments for solving second-order SDP relaxations arising from a diverse set of POPs. Our framework demonstrates state-of-the-art efficiency, scalability, and robustness in solving degenerate rank-one SDPs to high accuracy, even in the presence of millions of equality constraints.



∎

Computational Challenges in Solving High-order Relaxations
. The surging applications make it imperative to design computational tools for solving high-order (tight) SDP relaxations. However, high-order relaxations pose notorious challenges to existing SDP solvers. For this reason, in all applications above, the experiments were performed on solving (
POP
) problems of very small size,
e.g.,
the original (
POP
) has
\mathchar 29028
up to
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
for dense relaxations. The computational challenges mainly come from two aspects. In the first place, in stark contrast to Shor’s semidefinite relaxation, high order relaxations lead to SDPs with a large number of linear constraints, even if the dimension of the original (
POP
) is small. Taking the binary quadratic programming as an example (
i.e.,

\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\}
and
\mathchar 29040
is quadratic in (
POP
)), when
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720
, the number of equality constraints for
\mathchar 28948\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722
is
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28726\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28727\mathchar 28721
. In the second place, the resulting SDP is not only large, but also highly degenerate. Specifically, when the relaxation (
P
) is tight and admits rank-one optimal solutions, it necessarily fails the primal nondegeneracy condition
Alizadeh97MP-degeneracy
, which is crucial for ensuring numerical stability and fast convergence of existing algorithms
Alizadeh98SIOPT-ipm
;
Zhao2010
. Due to these two challenges, our experiments show that
no existing solver
can consistently solve semidefinite relaxations with rank-one solutions to a desired accuracy when
\mathchar 29037
is larger than
\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
. In particular, interior point methods (IPM), such as
SDPT3

Toh99OMS-sdpt3
and
MOSEK

mosek
, can only handle up to
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
before they run out of memory on an ordinary workstation.
3
3

3

When chordal sparsity exists in the SDP such that a large positive semidefinite constraint can be decomposed into multiple smaller ones, IPMs can be more scalable
Fukuda01siopt-sparsity
;
Zhang21MP-dualcliquetree
. However, for problems considered in this paper, there is no chordal sparsity.
First-order methods based on ADMM and conditional gradient method, such as
CDCS

Zheng20MP-CDCS
and
SketchyCGAL

Yurtsever21SIMDS-scalableSDP
, converge very slowly and cannot attain even modest accuracy for challenging instances. The best performing existing solver is
SDPNAL+

Yang15MPC-sdpnalplus
, which employs an augmented Lagrangian framework where the inner problem is solved inexactly by a semi-smooth Newton method with a conjugate gradient method (SSNCG), and hence has very good scalability. The problem with
SDPNAL+
is that, in the presence of large
\mathchar 29037
and degeneracy, solving the inexact SSN step involves solving a large and singular linear system of size
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29037
, and CG becomes incapable of computing the search direction with sufficient accuracy. A tempting alternative approach, since (
P
) has rank-one optimal solutions, is to apply the low-rank factorization method of Burer and Monteiro
Burer03MP-BMlowrank
,
i.e.,
solving the nonlinear optimization
\min_{\mathchar 29014\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29042}}}\{\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 28995\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29014\mathchar 29014^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\right\delimiter 0\ \delimiter 69640972\ {\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29014\mathchar 29014^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29026\}
for some small
\mathchar 29042
. We note that since
\mathchar 626_{\mathchar 29014}\delimiter 67273472\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 28993_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29014\mathchar 29014^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\right\delimiter 0\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28993_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 29014
, if
\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29014^{\mathchar 8511}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721
at the optimal solution
\mathchar 29014^{\mathchar 8511}
, then
\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28722\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28993_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 29014^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28993_{\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 29014^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84267779\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12828\mathchar 29037
no matter how large
\mathchar 29042
is. Therefore,
\mathchar 29014^{\mathchar 8511}
fails the linear independence constraint qualification (LICQ) and it may not be a KKT point, making it pessimistic for nonlinear programming solvers to find. In fact, successful applications of B-M factorization require
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12825\mathchar 29038
so that the dual multipliers can be obtained in closed-form from nonlinear programming solutions
Rosen19IJRR-sesync
;
Rosen20wafr-scalableSDP
;
Boumal16neurips-BM
. Nevertheless, exploiting low rankness and nonlinear programming tools is a great source of inspiration, and as we will show, our solver also exploits similar insights but in a fashion that is not subject to the large
\mathchar 29037
and degeneracy of problem (
P
).

Our Contribution.
In this paper, we contribute the first solver that can solve high-order SDP relaxations of (
POP
) to high accuracy in spite of large
\mathchar 29037
and degeneracy. Our solver employs a globally convergent
inexact projected gradient method
(iPGM) as the backbone for solving the convex SDP (
P
). Despite having favorable global convergence, the iPGM steps are typically
short
and lead to slow convergence, particularly in the presence of degeneracy. Therefore, we blend short iPGM steps with
long
rank-one steps generated by fast nonlinear programming (NLP) algorithms. The intuition is that, once we have achieved a sufficient amount of descent via iPGM, we can switch to NLP to perform local refinement to “jump” to a nearby rank-one point for rapid convergence. Due to this reason, we name our framework
SpecTrahedRon Inexact projected gradient Descent along vErtices
(
STRIDE
), which essentially strides along the rank-one vertices of the spectrahedron until the global minimum of the SDP is reached.

Before presenting the mathematical details of
STRIDE
in a general setup, let us
first introduce an informal version of
STRIDE
by running an illustrative example.

1.1
A Univariate Example

Consider minimizing a quartic polynomial subject to a single quartic equality constraint

\displaystyle\min_{\mathchar 29048\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}}}\ \ \left\{\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 28724}\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 28722\over\mathchar 28723}}\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28728\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28728\mathchar 29048\ \middle\delimiter 69640972\ \mathchar 29032\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28724\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\right\}\mathchar 24891

We now state our numerical algorithm that finds both
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 8511}
and
\mathchar 29050^{\mathchar 8511}

Initialization
. We use a standard nonlinear programming (NLP) solver (
e.g.,
an interior point method interfaced via
fmincon
in Matlab) to solve problem (
3
). We initialize at
\mathchar 29048^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
, and NLP converges to
\mathchar 29048^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722
, with a cost
\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12825\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28725\mathchar 314\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28723
(“

” in Fig.
1
(a)). Note that
\mathchar 29048^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722
is a strict local minimum because choosing a Lagrangian multiplier of “
\mathchar 28720
” for the constraint
\mathchar 29032
leads to
\mathchar 626\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
,
\mathchar 626^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28724

Lift and Descend
. The initialization step converges to a suboptimal solution. Unfortunately, it is very challenging, if not impossible, for NLP to be aware of this suboptimality, not to mention about escaping it. The SDP relaxation now comes into play. By lifting
\mathchar 29048^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}
to
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12349\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}

according to the SDP relaxation, we obtain the suboptimal rank-one vertex “

” in Fig.
1
(b) with
\mathchar 29050^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28724\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28728\delimiter 84267779^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
.
Although it is challenging to descend from
\mathchar 29048^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}
in POP (
3
), descending from
\mathchar 29050^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}
in the convex SDP (
1.1
) is relatively easy. We first take a step along the negative direction of the gradient to arrive at
\mathchar 29050^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 8235}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28955\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29050^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28955\mathchar 626\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28728\mathchar 28955\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28724\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28955\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28728\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28722\over\mathchar 28723}}\mathchar 28955\delimiter 84267779^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
for a given step size
\mathchar 28955\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720

back to the spectrahedron in Fig.

. Note that the projection step solves the problem
\min_{\mathchar 29050\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 28723}}}\{\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29016\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29016\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 8235}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 69645069^{\mathchar 28722}\ \delimiter 69640972\ \mathchar 29016\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12823\mathchar 28720\}

yields

”.

Rounding and Local Search
. The step we just performed is called
projected gradient descent
, and it is well known that iteratively performing this step guarantees convergence to the SDP optimal solution
Bertsekas99book-nlp
. However, the drawback is that it typically requires many iterations for the convergence to happen. Now we will show that, by leveraging local search back in POP (
3
), we can quickly arrive at the rank-one optimal point. The intuition is, from Fig.
1
(b), we can observe that
\bar{\mathchar 29050}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}
has moved “closer” to
\mathchar 29050^{\mathchar 8511}
, and hence maybe
\bar{\mathchar 29050}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}
already contains useful local information about
\mathchar 29050^{\mathchar 8511}
. Particularly, let us perform a spectral decomposition of the top-left
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 8706\mathchar 28722
block of
\mathchar 29016\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29050}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785
:

\displaystyle\left\delimiter 67482370\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathchar 28721&\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28725\\
\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28725&\mathchar 28723\mathchar 314\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28729\end{array}\right\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 28721}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\mathchar 24891\quad\text{with}

\displaystyle\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722\left\delimiter 67482370\begin{array}[]{c}\mathchar 28721\\
\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28728\mathchar 314\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28729\end{array}\right\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 24891\ \delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28729\left\delimiter 67482370\begin{array}[]{c}\mathchar 28721\\
\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722\end{array}\right\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 24891\ \mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28724\mathchar 314\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28724\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28729\mathchar 28726\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

where we have written
\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}
by normalizing their leading entries as
\mathchar 28721
, and ordered them such that
\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28722}
. We then generate two
hypotheses

\bar{\mathchar 29048}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28728\mathchar 314\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28729\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 314\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28722\delimiter 84054785
(“
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathchar 8719}
” in Fig.
1
(a)) and use NLP to perform local search starting from the hypotheses
\bar{\mathchar 29048}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722}
, respectively. Surprisingly, although
\bar{\mathchar 29048}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 28721}
still converges to
\mathchar 29048^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722
,
\bar{\mathchar 29048}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 28722}
converges to
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722
. We call this step
rounding and local search
.

Lift and Certify
. Now that NLP has visited both
\mathchar 29048^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}
and
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 8511}
, with
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 8511}
attaining a lower cost, we are certain that
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 8511}
is at least a better local minimum. To
certify
the global optimality of
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 8511}
, we need the SDP relaxation again. We lift
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 8511}
to
\mathchar 29050^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28722\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28724\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28728\delimiter 84267779^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
, and try to descend from
\mathchar 29050^{\mathchar 8511}
by performing another step of projected gradient descent.
However, because
\mathchar 29050^{\mathchar 8511}
is optimal, this step ends up back to
\mathchar 29050^{\mathchar 8511}
again,
i.e.,
no more descent is possible
Bertsekas99book-nlp
. Therefore, we conclude that
\mathchar 29050^{\mathchar 8511}
is optimal for SDP (
1.1
). Because
\mathchar 29016\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
is rank one, and
\mathchar 29040\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29050^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
, we can say that
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 8511}
is indeed globally optimal for the nonconvex POP (
3
) (
i.e.,
a numerical
certificate
of global optimality is obtained for
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 8511}
).

Despite the simplicity of this example, it clearly demonstrates the stark contrast between our algorithm
STRIDE
and existing approaches. While all existing methods solve the SDP relaxation
independently
from the original POP (
i.e.,
they relax the POP into an SDP and solve the SDP without
computationally
revisiting the POP), our method alternates between
local search
in POP and
global descent
in SDP, and the connection between the POP and the SDP is established by
lifting
and
rounding
. The advantages for doing so are threefold. (i)
Scalability
: Although the SDP may have millions of variables (
\mathchar 29038
and
\mathchar 29037
can grow rapidly), the original POP has only hundreds or thousands of variables (recall that
\mathchar 29028
is moderate) and an NLP solver can perform local search quickly (typically in a negligible amount of time compared to solving the SDP); (ii)
Degeneracy
: When the optimal SDP solution is rank one and degenerate, convergence of existing scalable SDP solvers (such as first-order methods) is typically very slow or even unachievable (
cf.
results in Section
5
). However, the original POP is much less sensitive to the degeneracy. Once the SDP iterate gets close to being optimal, NLP can quickly arrive at the global optimal solution. (iii)
Warm-starting
: Unlike IPMs, for which designing good initialization is relatively challenging, our algorithm easily benefits from warm-starting. As we will show in Section
5
, in many practical engineering applications, there exist powerful heuristics that find the globally optimal POP solution with high probability of success. In this case, our algorithm only needs to perform the last step of lifting and certification.

However, several questions need to be answered in order for our algorithm to be general and practical for large-scale problems.

Question 1

Since the projection onto a spectrahedron is itself an SDP and typically cannot be done exactly when
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29037
are large, how can one design a
globally convergent
solver for the original SDP (
P
) that can tolerate
inexactness
of the projection subproblem?

Question 2

With rounding and local search in the loop, is it still possible for the algorithm to be globally convergent?

Question 3

How can one design a
scalable and efficient
numerical algorithm to handle millions of constraints (
i.e.,

\mathchar 29037
) when computing the (inexact) projection onto the spectrahedron?

Question 4

While our univariate example shows that this framework works on the simple example above, will it work on more complicated problems arising in real world applications?

Section
2
answers Question
1
, where we design an
inexact
projected gradient method (iPGM) for solving a generic SDP pair (
P
)-(
D
), prove its global convergence, and provide complexity analysis. Although our results are inspired by
Jiang2012
, several nontrivial extensions are made. In answering Question
2
(Section
3
), we incorporate rounding and local search into iPGM and formally present
STRIDE
. In a nutshell,
STRIDE
follows the globally convergent trajectory driven by iPGM, but simultaneously probes long, but safeguarded, rank-one vertices of the spectrahedron generated from solutions of NLP, to seek rapid descent and convergence. Notably, we prove that, even with rounding and local search in the loop,
STRIDE
is guaranteed to converge to the optimal solution of (
P
)-(
D
). In Section
4
, we focus on solving the critical subproblem of projecting a given symmetric matrix onto the feasible set of (
P
) and provide an efficient answer to Question
3
. We propose a two-phase algorithm where phase one uses a
symmetric Gauss-Seidel based accelerated proximal gradient method
(sGS-APG) to generate a good initial point, and phase two applies a modified limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS) method to compute an accurate solution. This two-phase algorithm is simple, robust, easy to implement, and can scale to very large SDP problems with millions of constraints. Additionally, for the modified L-BFGS algorithm, we establish a novel convergence result where the objective function does not need to be at least twice continuously differentiable. Finally, we answer Question
4
by providing extensive numerical results in Section
5
. We apply
STRIDE
to solve (dense and sparse) second-order moment relaxations arising from a diverse set of POP problems and demonstrate its superior performance. We observe that
STRIDE
is the only solver that can consistently solve rank-one semidefinite relaxations to high accuracy (
e.g.,
KKT residuals below
\mathchar 28721\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28729}
) and it is up to
\mathchar 28721
-
\mathchar 28722
orders of magnitude faster than existing SDP solvers.

2
An inexact Projected Gradient Method for SDPs

In this section, we describe an inexact Projected Gradient Method (iPGM) for solving the SDP (
P
) and analyze its global convergence properties. Accelerating iPGM

3
STRIDE
: Accelerating iPGM by Nonlinear Programming

Despite being globally convergent, it may take many iterations for Algorithm
1
to converge to a solution of high accuracy, especially when the SDP (
P
) has large scale and the optimal solution
\mathchar 29016^{\mathchar 8511}
is low-rank and degenerate. Therefore, in this section, we propose to accelerate Algorithm
1
by rounding and local search, just as what we have shown in the simple numerical example in Fig.
1
of Section
1
. The intuition here is simple: when the SDP relaxation (
P
) is exact, there exist rank-one optimal solutions and they may be computed much more efficiently by performing local search in the low-dimensional (
POP
) with proper initialization.

With this intuition, we now develop the details of the acceleration scheme. At each iteration with current iterate
\mathchar 29016^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}
, we first follow (
16
) in Algorithm
1
and compute the projection

\overline{\mathchar 29016}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12825\mathchar 28677_{{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016^{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 28995\right\delimiter 84054785

under the inexactness conditions described in (
17
), where
\mathchar 28955_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720
is a given step size. Then by the analysis in Theorem
2.1
,
\overline{\mathchar 29016}^{\mathchar 29035}
is guaranteed to make certain progress towards optimality. However, the point
\overline{\mathchar 29016}^{\mathchar 29035}
may not be a promising candidate for the next iteration since it may not attain rapid convergence.
Motivated by the success of rounding and local search in the simple example in Fig.
1
, we propose to compute a potentially better candidate based on
\overline{\mathchar 29016}^{\mathchar 29035}
via the following steps:

(Rounding)

The answer to this question is quite natural –we accept

With this insight, we now introduce our algorithm

in Algorithm

\overline{\mathchar 29016}^{\mathchar 29035}
and previously accepted rank-one iterates. Notice that requiring
\widehat{\mathchar 29016}^{\mathchar 29035}

We now make a few remarks about Algorithm
2
(
STRIDE
).

The first remark is on the local search algorithm. In general, one can use any nonlinear programming method to perform local search on the original (
POP
) starting from an initial guess. A good choice is to use a solver that is based on a primal-dual interior point method, for which the global and local convergence properties are well studied
(
Jorge2006
, Chapter 19)
. Notice that even if the NLP solver can fail to converge to a feasible point of (
POP
), the safeguarding policy (
22
) ensures that all the rank-one points in
{\cal\mathchar 29014}
are feasible
for the SDP (P) (
i.e.,
a failed NLP solution will not be accepted). In many POPs arising from practical engineering applications, the constraint set typically defines a smooth manifold (see examples in Section
5
). In such cases, we prefer to use unconstrained optimization algorithms on the manifold as the local search method, for example the Riemannian trust region method that admits favorable global and local convergence properties
(
Absil09book-manifoldopt
, Chapter 7)
. A general interior point method for NLP is available through
fmincon
in Matlab (see
(
Jorge2006
, Section 19.9)
for other available software), while the Riemannian trust region method is available through
Manopt

manopt
. We emphasize here that the idea of using local search algorithms for accelerating iPGM is heuristic and only supported by numerical experiments in Section
5
. In other words, while we
guarantee global convergence of
STRIDE
for solving the SDP (
P
) (under mild technical assumptions), the amount of acceleration
gained by nonlinear programming may be
problem dependent and is mostly observed
empirically.
In the present paper, we are not able to establish conditions under which the local search algorithms can provide provably better rank-one candidates than the iterates generated by iPGM. We think this aspect deserves deeper future research. Nevertheless, the nice property of
STRIDE
is that,
even if we reject all the candidates provided by local search, global convergence is still guaranteed by taking the safeguarded iPGM steps.

Next, we comment on the number of eigenvectors to round. In
STRIDE
, the hyperparameter
\mathchar 29042
decides how many eigenvectors to round and how many hypotheses to generate at each iteration. Since the NLP solver performs local search very quickly, it is affordable to choose a large
\mathchar 29042
. However, we empirically observed that choosing
\mathchar 29042
between
\mathchar 28722
and
\mathchar 28725
is sufficient for finding the global optimal solution.

Lastly, we provide a possible extension to
STRIDE
. The careful reader may have noticed that Algorithm
2
uses the general Algorithm iPGM
without
acceleration. We emphasize here that the accelerated version studied in
Jiang2012
can also be used as the backbone of
STRIDE
with the same global convergence property. The reason we only implement the unaccelerated version is because empirically, with a proper warmstart (described in Section
3.1
), we observe that Algorithm
2
converges in
\mathchar 28721
or
\mathchar 28722
iterations, just like what we have shown in the simple univariate example in Section
1
. Therefore, the acceleration scheme would not have major improvement on the efficiency of Algorithm
2
.

3.1
Initialization

STRIDE
requires an initial guess
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011^{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 8706{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}\mathchar 8706\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}
as described in Algorithm

We first mention that, for many POPs arising from engineering applications, practitioners often have designed powerful heuristics for solving the POPs based on their specific domain knowledge. By heuristics we mean algorithms that can find the
globally optimal
POP solutions with very high probability of success, but
cannot
provide a certificate of global optimality (or suboptimality). Therefore, when such heuristics exist, we could use them to generate
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 28720}
for the (
POP
) and perform a rank-one lifting of
\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 28720}
to form
\mathchar 29016^{\mathchar 28720}

\mathchar 29016^{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048^{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
, where
\delimiter 69640972
is the lifting monomials. In Section
5.3
, we demonstrate the effectiveness of one such heuristic in speeding up

on a computer vision application.

4
Solving the Projection Subproblem

Recall that the feasible set of (
P
) is
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}\mathchar 12349\left\{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 12906{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29026\mathchar 24891\;\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12823\mathchar 28720\right\}
. In this section, we aim at computing the projection onto
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}
efficiently since it is required at each iteration in
STRIDE
(Algorithm
2
). Note that since there are
\mathchar 29037
linear equality constraints defining
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}
with
\mathchar 29037
possibly as large as a few millions, the projection algorithm has to be scalable. To this end, we propose a two-phase algorithm. In phase one (Section
4.1
), we use an sGS-based accelerated proximal gradient method to generate a reasonably good initial point for the purpose of warm starting. Then in phase two (Section
4.2
), we apply a modified version of the classical limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS) method to compute a highly accurate solution.

Formally, given a point
\mathchar 29018\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}
, the projection problem is described as finding the closest point in
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}
with respect to
\mathchar 29018

\min_{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}}\left\{{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29016\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29018\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 28998}_{\mathchar 29008}\right\}\mathchar 314

(24)

Notice that the feasible set
{\cal\mathchar 28998}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29008}}
is a spectrahedron defined by the intersection of two convex sets, namely the hyperplane
\left\{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 12346\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29026\right\}
and the PSD cone
\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}
. Therefore, a natural idea is to apply Dykstra’s projection (see
e.g.,

Patrick2011
) for generating an approximate solution to (
24
) by alternating the projection onto the hyperplane and the projection onto the PSD cone, both of which are easy to compute. However, Dykstra’s projection is known to have slow convergence rate and it may take too many iterations until a satisfactory approximate projection is found. In this paper, instead of solving (
24
) directly, we consider its dual problem for which we can handle more efficiently.

It is not difficult to write down the Lagrangian dual problem (ignoring the constant term
\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29018\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}
and converting “

\min_{\mathchar 29015\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28952}\left\{{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29015\mathchar 8235\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 28952\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29018\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29026\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28952\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 12906\mathchar 28952\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 24891\;\mathchar 29015\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}\right\}\mathchar 314

(25)

)-(

Problem (

It is desirable to apply a proximal-type method for problem (
29

\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\delimiter 84054785

\mathchar 29041_{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\delimiter 84054785

\mathchar 29041_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29031

\mathchar 29030\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29031
near the current iterate, and (ii) minimizing
\mathchar 29041_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29031
can be solved efficiently. In particular, one needs to choose an appropriate positive definite mapping
{\cal\mathchar 29000}_{\mathchar 29035}
(
\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28721

Theorem 4.2

Suppose that
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29015^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28952^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 8235}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 8706\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29037}

) and that
\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}

\{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29015^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28952^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\}

\mathchar 29027\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720

\mathchar 28720\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29015^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28952^{\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29015^{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28952^{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820{{\mathchar 29027\over\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}}}\mathchar 314

The proof of Theorem

can be taken directly from
(

, Proposition 2)
. Note that the proposed method in this subsection is in fact a direct application of the one proposed in
(
Li2019
, Section 4)
which is designed for a more general convex composite quadratic programming model with multiple blocks. Moreover,
(
Li2019
, Section 4)
also investigates the inexact computation for the corresponding subproblem. Thus, when
\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}
cannot be factorized efficiently, one may apply an iterative solver
for solving the linear systems in (

) of Algorithm
4
. We omit the details here since for the applications studied in this paper, factorizing
\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}
can always be done efficiently due to the sparsity of
{\cal\mathchar 28993}
.
We further remark that for the computation of
\mathchar 29015^{\mathchar 29035}
in (
37
),
we can make use of the expected low-rank property of the projection
\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 8235}^{\mathchar 29038}}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\tilde{\mathchar 28952}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785
corresponding to the primal variable.
Again, we use the subroutine
dsyevx
in LAPACK to compute
only the positive eigen-pairs of
{\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\tilde{\mathchar 28952}^{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29018}
.

4.2
Phase Two: A Modified Limited-memory BFGS Method

Even though the sGS-APG method, as presented in Algorithm
4
, converges as
\mathchar 29007\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 29035^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
in terms of objective value, the constant
\mathchar 29027
depends on the distance between the initial point and the optimal solution, which can be quite large if the initial point
is not well chosen. Therefore, it may require many iterations to reach a satisfactory solution when the initial point is far way from the optimal solution set.
Moreover, the sGS-APG method may not have a fast
local linear convergent property since the problem (
25
) is not strongly convex.
To speed up the computation of the dual projection problem (
25
), we now propose a modified version of the classical limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS) method that is warm-started by the solution from Algorithm
4
.

To proceed, we observe that, fixing the unconstrained
\mathchar 28952
, the dual projection problem (
25
) becomes finding the closest
\mathchar 29015\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}
to the matrix
\mathchar 8704\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 28952\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785
and admits a closed-form expression

\displaystyle\mathchar 29015\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 28952\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29018\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

(38)

As a result, problem (
25
) can be further simplified, after inserting (
38
), as

\min_{\mathchar 28952\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}}\quad\mathchar 28958\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28952\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 8235}^{\mathchar 29038}}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 28952\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29026\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28952\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 24891

(39)

with the gradient of
\mathchar 28958\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28952\delimiter 84054785
given as

\mathchar 626\mathchar 28958\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28952\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 8235}^{\mathchar 29038}}\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 28952\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026\mathchar 314

(40)

We have used the following equality in getting (
39
)

\displaystyle\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 28952\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29018\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 28952\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29018\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 28952\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785

due to the Moreau identity
(
Malick06sva-projsdp
, Theorem 2.2)
.
Thus, if
\mathchar 28952^{\mathchar 8511}
is an optimal solution for problem (
39
), then we can recover
\mathchar 29015^{\mathchar 8511}
from (
38
). Formulating the dual projection problem as (
39
) has appeared multiple times; see, for instances
Zhao2010
;
Malick09siopt-sdpregularization
.
We note that the function
\mathchar 28958\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\delimiter 84054785
is smooth but not twice continuously
differentiable, and it is convex but not strongly convex.

Now that (
39
) is an unconstrained convex minimization problem in
\mathchar 28952\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29037}
with gradient given in (
40
),
many efficient algorithms are available for solving the problem, such as (accelerated) gradient descent methods
Nesterov18book-convexopt
, nonlinear conjugate gradient methods
Dai99siopt-ncg
, quasi-Newton methods
Jorge2006
and the semi-smooth Newton method
Zhao2010
. For this paper, we decide to propose a modified limited-memory BFGS (L-BFGS) method because L-BFGS is easy to implement, can handle very large unconstrained optimization problems, and is typically the “the algorithm of choice” for large-scale problems.
Empirically, we observed that our proposed L-BFGS warm-started by sGS-APG is efficient and robust for various class of applications (shown in Section
5
). This observation also supports the discussions made in
(
Jorge2006
, Chapter 7)
. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work that demonstrates the effectiveness of L-BFGS, or in general quasi-Newton methods, on solving large and challenging SDPs.
4
4

4

From our extensive numerical trials, we found that accelerated gradient descent, nonlinear conjugate gradient, and semismooth Newton with CG iterative solver fail to give satisfactory performance, especially when
\mathchar 29037
is very large.

The template for the modified L-BFGS method is presented in Algorithm
5
.

5
Applications and Numerical Experiments

In this section, we conduct numerical experiments by using
STRIDE
to solve SDP relaxations for several important classes of POPs. In Section
5.1
and
5.2
, we solve
dense
second-order moment relaxation of random
binary quadratic programming
problems, and random
quartic optimization problems over the unit square
, both with increasing number of variables
\mathchar 29028
. We demonstrate, for the first time, that the relaxation is always tight for the instances we have generated up to
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720
. In Section
5.3
and
5.4
, we solve
sparse
second-order moment relaxations coming from two engineering applications, namely an
outlier-robust Wahba problem
that underpins many computer vision applications, and a
nearest structured rank deficient matrix problem
that finds extensive applications in control, statistics, computer algebra, among others. We demonstrate that, with a sparse relaxation scheme, we can globally solve POP problems with
\mathchar 29028
up to
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
, far beyond the reach of SDP solvers used in the corresponding engineering literature. When solving the outlier-robust Wahba problem in Section
5.3
, we additionally show that (i) leveraging domain-specific POP heuristics for primal initialization can further speed up
STRIDE
by 2-3 times, and (ii)
STRIDE
can
certifiably optimally
solve two real applications of the outlier-robust Wahba problem, namely image stitching and scan matching. Our experiments show that
STRIDE
is the
only
solver that can consistently solve rank-one tight semidefinite relaxations to high accuracy (
e.g.,
KKT residuals below
\mathchar 28721\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28729}
), in the presence of millions of equality constraints.

Before presenting each application, let us describe the details about implementation and experimental setup.

Implementation
. We implement the
STRIDE
Algorithm
2
in MATLAB R2020a, with core subroutines, such as projection onto the PSD cone, implemented in C for efficiency. Our implementation is available at

https://github.com/MIT-SPARK/STRIDE

and also supports SDP problems with multiple PSD blocks (hence, it can also handle relaxations of (
POP
) problems with inequality constraints).

Note that different from generic SDP solvers, we also implement the rounding, local search and lifting procedures required in Step 2 of Algorithm
2
, for each POP. Since these procedures are problem dependent, we will describe them in the corresponding subsections.

Stopping Conditions
.
To measure the feasibility and optimality at a given approximate solution

residues:

Baseline Solvers.

We compare
STRIDE
with a diverse set of existing SDP solvers. We choose
SDPT3

Toh99OMS-sdpt3
and
MOSEK

mosek
as representative interior point methods;
CDCS

Zheng20MP-CDCS
and
SketchyCGAL

Yurtsever21SIMDS-scalableSDP
as representative first-order methods; and
SDPNAL+

Yang15MPC-sdpnalplus
as a representative method that combines first-order and second-order Newton-type methods. For
SDPT3
and
MOSEK
, we use default parameters. For

, we use the

\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
iterations instead of the default homogeneous self-dual embedding solver because we found
sos
to typically perform better.
For
SketchyCGAL
, we use the default parameters with sketching size
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
. We set the maximum runtime to be
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
seconds and maximum number of iterations to be
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
. For
SDPNAL+
, we use
\mathchar 28721\mathrm{\mathchar 29029}{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28728}
as the tolerance, and we run it for maximum
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
iterations and
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
seconds. For very large problems (
e.g.,

\mathchar 29037
above one million), we increase the maximum runtime of

and
SDPNAL+
, which will be described in relevant subsections.

Hardware.
All experiments are performed on a Linux PC with 12-core Intel i9-7920X CPU@2.90GHz and 128GB RAM.

5.1
Binary quadratic programming

Consider minimizing a
quadratic
polynomial over the
\mathchar 29028
-dimensional binary cube

\min_{\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29028}\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29027\mathchar 24891\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}\right\delimiter 0

(BQP)

where
\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12346{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29028}}\mathchar 12833{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}}}
is the vector of monomials in
\mathchar 29048
of degree up to 2, and
\mathchar 29027\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}}}
contains the coefficients of all monomials. Problem (
BQP
) is a classical NP-hard combinatorial problem with examples such as the maximum cut (MAXCUT) problem
Goemans95JACM-maxcut
, the
\mathchar 28720
-
\mathchar 28721
knapsack problem
Helmberg00JCO-SDPKnapsack
, the number partitioning problem
Mertens06CCSP-npp
;
Gamarnik21arxiv-npp
, and the linear quadratic regulator control problem with binary inputs
Wu18TAC-LQRswitchedsystems
. It is well known that the standard Shor’s semidefinite relaxation for (
BQP
) is typically not tight,
e.g.,
in MAXCUT problems, and hence a globally optimal solution cannot be obtained with an optimality certificate (albeit a lower bound can be obtained).

We consider the
second-order
dense moment relaxation for (
BQP
), which creates a
positive semidefinite
moment matrix
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
, using which the cost function in (
BQP
) can be written as
\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 28995\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29016\right\delimiter 0
. The moment matrix
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}}
contains all the monomials in
\mathchar 29048
of degree up to 4, hence is a linear subspace of
\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}}
with dimension
\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28724}
. As a result,
\mathchar 29016
must satisfy
\mathchar 29037_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29037}}\mathchar 12349\mathfrak{\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28724}
linearly independent equality constraints, referred to as the
moment
constraints. In addition,
\mathchar 29016
must satisfy
redundant
equality constraints, obtained by the fact that since
\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
, it also holds
\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
for each
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29028
. This leads to a total of
\mathchar 29037_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29036\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8706\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}
linear equality constraints.
Last but not least, the top-left entry of
\mathchar 29016
is equal to 1 due to the leading element of
\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}
is 1 (the zero-order monomial).
6
6

6

The reader can refer to
Mai20arXiv-spectralPOP
or our code for details about generating SDP data
{\cal\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29026\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28995
.
Therefore, the second-order relaxation for (
BQP
) leads to an SDP with size

\displaystyle\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12349\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\quad\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29037_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29037}}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29037_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29036\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29027}}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12349\mathfrak{\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28724}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8706\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891

(47)

which grows rapidly with
\mathchar 29028
. Lasserre
Lasserre01ICIPCO-SDPbinary
showed that the second-order relaxation is empirically tight on a set of 50 randomly generated MAXCUT problems. However, due to the limitation of interior point methods back then, the experiments were performed on problems with small size
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
.

In this paper, we aim to solve (
BQP
) instances with much larger
\mathchar 29028
. We generate random instances of (
BQP
) by sampling the coefficients vector
\mathchar 29027
from the standard zero-mean Gaussian distribution,
i.e.,

\mathchar 29027_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12824{\cal\mathchar 29006}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}
. At
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720\}
, we randomly generate three instances each and solve the second-order moment relaxation using
SDPT3
,
MOSEK
,
SDPNAL+
,
CDCS
,
SketchyCGAL
, and
STRIDE
.
For
STRIDE
, we use the standard
fmincon
interface in Matlab with an interior point solver (supplied with analytical objective and constraint gradients) as the
nlp
method. For
rounding
hypotheses from the moment matrix, we follow

\displaystyle\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\mathchar 24891\quad\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12832\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 24891\quad\bar{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29031\mathchar 29038}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

(48)

which first performs a spectral decomposition of
\mathchar 29016
with
\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12821\dots\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29038}
in non-increasing order, then normalizes the
\mathchar 29033
-th eigenvector
\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}
so that its leading element is
\mathchar 28721
, and finally generates
\bar{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}
by taking the sign of the elements of
\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}
that correspond to the order-one monomials. We round
\mathchar 29042\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28725
hypotheses using the first
\mathchar 28725
eigenvectors (
\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\dots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28725
).
In order to compute
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}
, we set
\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29026}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29038
because the diagonal entries of
\mathchar 29016
are all equal to 1.

Table
1
gives the numerical results of different solvers. We make the following observations. (i) We first look at the performance of interior point methods (IPMs,
SDPT3
and
MOSEK
). For small-scale problems (
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
), IPMs can solve the SDPs efficiently to high accuracy (
e.g.,
around
\mathchar 28721
second). For medium-scale problems (
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
), although IPMs can still obtain solutions with high accuracy, the computational time starts to grow significantly (
e.g.,

\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
-
\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
seconds). Moreover, for large-scale problems (
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
), IPMs cannot be executed on ordinary workstations due to intensive memory consumption. The fundamental challenge of IPMs lies in solving large and dense linear systems (
i.e.,
the
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29037
Schur complement system) at each iteration. Although it is possible to use iterative solvers to solve the linear system
toh04siopt-iterativesolver
, they are known to suffer from slow convergence as interior-point iterates approach optimality. (ii) First-order solvers (
CDCS
and
SketchyCGAL
) can solve problems to medium or low accuracy for
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
, but their runtime can be worse than IPMs (although
CDCS
is faster than
MOSEK
for
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
, its accuracy is orders of magnitude worse than
MOSEK
). However, first-order methods are indeed advantageous in terms of memory consumption and they can still be executed for problems with up to
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720
. Nevertheless, they are not able to compute POP solutions of certified global optimality within reasonable time, as shown by the nonzero
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}
in Table
1
(
i.e.,
they are not able to show that the relaxation is indeed tight). This phenomenon further stresses the challenge for solving degenerate rank-one SDP relaxations. We also observe that, between
CDCS
and
SketchyCGAL
,
CDCS
seems to perform much better for such problems. This suggests that sketching may not be the best choice for degenerate SDPs with large
\mathchar 29037
. (iii)
SDPNAL+
has the best performance among existing solvers for (
BQP
). It can solve (
BQP
) instances to certified global optimality for up to
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28720
, and it is over
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
times faster than
MOSEK
and
CDCS
when
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
. However, when
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720
and
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720
,
SDPNAL+
cannot solve the SDPs to sufficient accuracy (within
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720\mathchar 28720
seconds), and hence the POP solution cannot be certified as globally optimal (
cf.
the nonzero
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}
). (iv) Finally, we look at the performance of our solver
STRIDE
. We observe that
STRIDE
solved all the SDPs to high accuracy, certified the global optimality of the POP solutions, and demonstrated the tightness of the SDP relaxations (
cf.
the numerically zero
\mathchar 28945_{\mathchar 29043}
). For small and medium problems (
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
and
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
),
STRIDE
attains accuracy that is comparable to
MOSEK
, while being about
\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
times faster when
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720
. For large problems (
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720
),
STRIDE
attains accuracy that is superior to
SDPNAL+
, while being
\mathchar 28725
-
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720
times faster when
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720
. At
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720
with
\mathchar 29037
over a million,
STRIDE
becomes the only solver that can obtain solutions of high accuracy.

5.2
Quartic programming on the sphere

Consider minimizing a
quartic
polynomial over the
\mathchar 29028
-dimension unit sphere

\min_{\mathchar 29048\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}}\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29027\mathchar 24891\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28724}\right\delimiter 0

(Q4S)

where
\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28724}\mathchar 12346{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29028}}\mathchar 12833{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28724}}}
is the vector of monomials in
\mathchar 29048
of degree up to 4, and
\mathchar 29027\mathchar 12850{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28724}}}
is the vector of known coefficients. Problem (
Q4S
) is known to be NP-hard with important examples such as computing the largest stable set of a graph
(
deKlerk08CEJOR-simplexhypercubesphere
, Theorem 3.4)
, computing the
\mathchar 28722\mathchar 12833\mathchar 28724
norm of a matrix
Barak12ACM-hypercontractivity
, and the best separable state problem in quantum information theory
Doherty04PRA-quantumseparability
. See
Fang20MP-SOSsphere
;
Ling10SIOPT-biquadratic
and references therein for a thorough discussion about problem (
Q4S
).

Here we consider the dense second-order (also the lowest order) moment relaxation of (
Q4S
) and numerically show that they are indeed tight and admit rank-one solutions. By following the same relaxation scheme as in Section
5.1
(
i.e.,
build the moment matrix
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
and add equality constraints), we can count the size of the SDP relaxation to be

\displaystyle\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12349\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\quad\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12349\mathfrak{\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28724}\mathchar 8235\bar{\mathchar 29028}_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\mathchar 314

(49)

At each
\mathchar 29028\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28722\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28723\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28724\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28725\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28726\mathchar 28720\}
, we generate three random instances of (
Q4S
) by drawing
\mathchar 29027
from the standard normal distribution. We solve the resulting SDP using
SDPT3
,
MOSEK
,
CDCS
,
SketchyCGAL
,
SDPNAL+
, and
STRIDE
.
For
STRIDE
, we exploit the manifold structure of the sphere constraint and adopt
Manopt

manopt
with a trust region solver as the
nlp
method. One can also treat (
Q4S
) as a standard nonlinear programming and solve it with
fmincon
, but we found that
Manopt
is faster and more robust for this problem. To generate hypotheses for
nlp
from the moment matrix, we follow

\displaystyle\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12349\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\mathchar 24891\quad\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12832\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 68408078\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 24891\quad\bar{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349{{\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779\over\left\delimiter 0\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779\right\delimiter 0}}\mathchar 24891

(50)

which first performs a spectral decomposition of
\mathchar 29016
with
\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12821\dots\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29038}
in non-increasing order, then normalizes the
\mathchar 29033
-th eigenvector
\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}
so that its leading element is
\mathchar 28721
, and finally generates
\bar{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}
by projecting the elements of
\delimiter 69640972_{\mathchar 29033}
that correspond to the order-one monomials onto the unit sphere. We generate
\mathchar 29042\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28725
hypotheses using the first
\mathchar 28725
eigenvectors. A valid upper bound
\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29026}
on
\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\right\delimiter 84054785
can be obtained as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\right\delimiter 84054785&\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29044\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 28722}\\
&\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8235\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29028}\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 28721\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29028}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28722\mathchar 8235\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29028}\mathchar 29048_{\mathchar 29033}^{\mathchar 28722}\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28723\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29005_{\mathchar 29026}\mathchar 314\end{split}
